Representation Learning for Equivariant Inference with Guarantees
Daniel Ordoñez-Apraez, Vladimir R. Kostić, Alek Fröhlich, Vivien Brandt, Karim Lounici, Massimiliano Pontil

Abstract
In many real-world applications of regression, conditional probability estimation, and uncertainty quantification, exploiting symmetries rooted in physics or geometry can dramatically improve generalization and sample efficiency. While geometric deep learning has made empirical advances by incorporating symmetry and geometry priors, less attention has been given to statistical learning guarantees. In this paper, we introduce an equivariant representation learning framework that simultaneously addresses regression, conditional probability estimation, and uncertainty quantification while providing first-of-its-kind non-asymptotic statistical learning guarantees. Grounded in operator and group representation theory, our framework approximates the spectral decomposition of the conditional expectation operator, building representations that are both equivariant and disentangled along independent symmetry quotient groups. Empirical evaluations on synthetic datasets and real-world robotics applications confirm the potential of our approach, matching or outperforming existing equivariant baselines in regression while providing well-calibrated uncertainty estimates.

affiliationnotice:
Equal contribution

1
Introduction

A central problem in machine learning is modeling conditional probabilities—understanding how the distribution of a target variable
{\mathbf{y}}
changes with an observed variable
{\mathbf{x}}
. This underpins robust reasoning under uncertainty in critical applications such as medicine, finance, robotics, and physics
(
Izbicki, 2025
;
Smith, 2013
;
Wasserman, 2007
)
. However, estimating conditional distributions remains challenging in high-dimensional settings without strong inductive biases
(
Scott, 1991
;
Nagler & Czado, 2016
;
Izbicki & B. Lee, 2017
)
.

Symmetry priors, in the form of principled assumptions about invariance or equivariance in the underlying data-generating process, offer a compelling way to reduce sample complexity and improve generalization
(
Kashinath et al., 2021
;
Wang, 2021
;
Bronstein et al., 2021
;
Elesedy, 2023
)
. These priors naturally arise in inference tasks in chemistry and particle physics
(
Dresselhaus et al., 2007
)
, set-&-graph structured data
(
Bronstein et al., 2021
)
, computer graphics
(
Mitra et al., 2013
;
Kovar et al., 2002
)
, and dynamical systems with group-invariant/equivariant laws of motion, which are ubiquitous in fields like physics
(
Dresselhaus et al., 2007
)
, fluid dynamics
(
Olver, 1993
)
, and robotics
(
Ordoñez-Apraez et al., 2025
;
Zhu et al., 2022
)
.

Figure 1
:

Left
: Test set sample efficiency for
\G
-equivariant regression (MSE vs. training samples) when predicting the
\G
-equivariant linear and angular momentum of a quadruped robot’s center of mass (
CoM
) from noisy joint positions and velocities.
Right
: Uncertainty quantification via
\G
-equivariant prediction of
90\%

CIs
(light-red area) for the robot’s instantaneous work
U_{t}
and kinetic energy
T_{t}
during locomotion over rough terrain for our method (
eNCP
) and competitors. The figure shows a trajectory with a strong initial disturbance, where blue markers denote samples within the predicted CI and red markers denote those outside. Note that only
eNCP
is able to predict well-calibrated CI intervals that cover both the disturbance and recovery phases.

Over the past few years,
Geometric Deep Learning (GDL)
has produced a rich ecosystem of architectures that encode symmetries, achieving strong empirical performance across various supervised
(
Bronstein et al., 2021
;
Weiler et al., 2023
;
van der Pol et al., 2020
)
and unsupervised tasks
(
Dangovski et al., 2022
;
Keurti et al., 2023
;
Wang et al., 2024a
)
. However, the field remains focused on application specific designs and architectural innovation, with limited understanding of how symmetry priors can be leveraged to
learn representations with provable generalization guarantees
.

In this work, we take a different route: rather than proposing new architectures or solving specific inference tasks, we ask
how to systematically learn symmetry-aware representations that best capture conditional structure in the data
. Specifically, how should equivariant networks be trained so that their learned features reveal conditional distributions, and how does the quality of these representations affect performance in downstream tasks such as regression, conditional probability estimation, and uncertainty quantification?

To answer these questions, we connect two fields rarely studied together: spectral contrastive learning
(
Zou et al., 2013
)
, a self-supervised approach for learning deep representations via operator-theoretic models of conditional expectations
(
Tsai et al., 2021
;
Kostic et al., 2024a
)
, and
GDL

(
Bronstein et al., 2021
)
, which imposes symmetry and geometry priors through architectural constraints in
Neural Networks (NNs)
. Our approach clarifies how symmetry constraints shape the representation space and improve generalization, opening new avenues for exchange between these fields. Concretely, we show that our method outperforms existing
GDL
techniques on regression tasks and provides reliable uncertainty quantification for robot locomotion (
Figure

1
).

Contributions

(1) Methodological framework: We introduce
Equivariant Neural Conditional Probability (eNCP)
, the first framework to combine equivariant neural networks with operator-theoretic estimation of conditional distributions. (2) Task-agnostic representation learning: We show that any
\G
-equivariant architecture can be used to learn
disentangled, symmetry-respecting representations
that generalize across diverse downstream inference tasks. (3) Learning guarantees: By linking the representation quality directly to sample complexity, we provide the
first non-asymptotic statistical learning guarantees
for equivariant regression and symmetry-aware conditional probability estimation and uncertainty quantification. (4) Empirical results:
1
1

1

Code available at

github.com/Danfoa/symm_rep_learn

On both synthetic and real-world robotics tasks,
eNCP
consistently outperforms baselines, including contrastive methods
Neural Conditional Probability (NCP)

(
Kostic et al., 2024a
)
and current equivariant models. In particular,
eNCP
achieves state-of-the-art performance in the challenging robotics task of contact force estimation.

Paper Structure

Section

2
reviews modeling conditional probabilities with linear operators and
NCP
.
Section

3
formally presents the symmetry priors we consider.
Section

4
introduces our
eNCP
learning framework.
Section

5
outlines our theoretical learning guarantees.
Section

6
showcases experiments on synthetic and real-world data. Furthermore, because the paper involves complex notation from probability, operator theory, and group theory, the appendices include a glossary of notation (
Appendix

A
) as well as detailed expositions on representation theory (
Appendix

H
), symmetric function spaces (
Appendix

I
), and equivariant linear operators (
Appendix

J
). Finally,
Appendix

B
offers an in-depth discussion of related work, contrasting our work with the literature across these rich fields.

2
Background

Figure 2
:

Left:

NCP
’s bilinear
NN
architecture.
Right:

eNCP
’s
\G
-equivariant bilinear
NN
architecture, featuring
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}
and
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}
as
\G
-equivariant
NNs
and
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
as a
\G
-equivariant block-diagonal matrix. Each block is equivariant to a quotient group
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\subseteq\G
and is constrained to have singular spaces of dimension at least
d_{{k}}
—the minimal dimension for a representation of the action of
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
.

We briefly review the operator-theoretic framework for modeling conditional probabilities, which underpins both
NCP
and our proposed
eNCP
method. We denote a random variable by
{\mathbf{x}}
, its realizations by
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, its probability distribution by
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}})
, and measure by
\mux
. Expectations are written as
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}[f({\mathbf{x}})]=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f({\bm{x}})\,P_{\mathbf{x}}(d{\bm{x}})
. The same notation applies to other random variables such as
{\mathbf{y}}
.

Modeling of Conditional Probabilities

Kostic et al. (2024a)
proposed to model conditional probabilities by approximating the
conditional expectation operator

(
Baker, 1973
;
Song et al., 2009
;
Ryu et al., 2024
)
,
\CE\colon\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}\to\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
, a linear integral operator acting on the Hilbert spaces
\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}:=\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\mux}$}({\mathcal{X}},\R)
and
\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}:=\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\muy}$}({\mathcal{Y}},\R)
of square-integrable functions of the random variables
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
, respectively. The action of this operator on any function
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
returns the function’s conditional expectation:

\displaystyle({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}\!=\!{\bm{x}}]:=\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}})P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})

(1)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}})\tfrac{P_{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{y}},d{\bm{x}})}{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}})}=\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}})\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}}),

where
P_{{\mathbf{y}}\mid{\mathbf{x}}}
is the conditional probability measure, and
\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}){:=}\frac{\muxy(d{\bm{x}},d{\bm{y}})}{\mux(d{\bm{x}})\,\muy(d{\bm{y}})}
is the
PMD

(
Sugiyama et al., 2012
)
kernel defining
\CE
, obtained as the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the joint measure to the product of marginal measures (see
Figures

3
and
G
).

The conditional expectation operator is significant because it provides an infinite-dimensional linear model—in a nonlinear representation space—for computing conditional probabilities and expectations. To see this, note that for any
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
and any measurable set
{\mathbb{B}}\subset{\mathcal{Y}}
we have that:

\small\begin{split}\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathbb{B}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}):=\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}({\bm{y}}){P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}}(d{\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})=[\CE\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}]({\bm{x}}),\;\;\text{and}\\
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}\!=\!{\bm{x}}]:=[\CE{\mathbf{y}}]({\bm{x}}).\hskip 18.49988pt\end{split}

(2)

Therefore, to estimate conditional probabilities and expectations,
NCP
seeks the best finite-dimensional approximation of
\CE
. As we explain next, this gives rise to a representation learning problem
(
Oord et al., 2018
)
, in which the optimal representations of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
are given by the top left and right singular functions of
\CE

(
Kostic et al., 2024b
;
Ryu et al., 2024
)
.

Spectral Representation Learning

The problem of approximating the conditional expectation operator
\CE
as a rank-
r
operator
{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
with matrix representation
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\in\R^{r\times r}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\argmin_{\nnParams}

\displaystyle\|\CE{-}{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}(\pmd({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}))^{2},

(3)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})\!=\!1\;\text{ and }\;\rank({\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}})\leq r+1.

The optimal solution, denoted
{\mathsf{E}}_{\star}
, is the
r
-truncated
Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)
of
\CE

(
Eckart & Young, 1936
;
Weidmann, 2012
;
Baker, 1973
)
, namely

\displaystyle({\bm{x}})=

\displaystyle{\textstyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{r}}\sigma_{i}\,{\langle f,v_{i}\rangle}_{\muy}u_{i}({\bm{x}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with}

(4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}u_{i}({\bm{x}})=[\CE v_{i}]({\bm{x}}),\;\forall i\in[r],

where
(\sigma_{i},u_{i},v_{i})
denotes the
i^{\text{th}}
singular value and left/right singular functions of
\CE
, with
(\sigma_{0}{=}1,u_{0}{=}\mathds{1}_{\mux},v_{0}{=}\mathds{1}_{\muy})
being the constant functions supported on
\mux
and
\muy
.

Consequently,

NCP
parameterizes

{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
by a bilinear model

\pmd_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=\mathds{1}_{\mux}({\bm{x}})\mathds{1}_{\muy}({\bm{y}})+\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}})^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{y}})
, composed of two encoder
NNs

\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\R^{r}
and
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}\colon{\mathcal{Y}}\to\R^{r}
that aim to approximate the
span
of the top
r
(non-constant) left and right singular functions of
\CE
. See
Figure

2
-left.

As
\pmd
is generally unavailable analytically, (
3
) is solved via the regularized
contrastive low-rank (CLoRa)
loss:

\small\begin{split}\small\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\nnParams)&\,=\,-2\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})+\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{2}\\
&+2\gamma\big(\|\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}})\|_{F}^{2}+\|\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\,\|\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams})-{\bm{I}}_{r}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams})-{\bm{I}}_{r}\|_{F}^{2}\big),\end{split}

(5)

where the first two regularization terms center the learned representations, ensuring that
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){\approx}1

(
Kostic et al., 2024a
)
, while the last two enforce approximate orthonormality of the learned bases in
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}:=\operatorname{span}(\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams})\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
and
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}:=\operatorname{span}(\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams})\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}

(
Izbicki & B. Lee, 2017
)
.
A key property of
NCP
is that the learned representations enables reliable regression and conditional probability estimation—and thus uncertainty quantification—via (
2
).

3
Problem Formulation

Figure 3
:

Example of symmetric random variables
({\textnormal{x}},{\textnormal{y}})\sim{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\subset\R\times\R
, whose marginals
\mathbb{P}({\textnormal{x}})
and
\mathbb{P}({\textnormal{y}})
; joint
\mathbb{P}({\textnormal{x}},{\textnormal{y}})
; and conditional
\mathbb{P}({\textnormal{y}}|{\textnormal{x}})
distributions are invariant to reflections of the data:
g_{r}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}x=-x
and
g_{r}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}y=-y
, where
g_{r}
denotes the reflection element of the reflection symmetry group
\mathbb{C}_{2}:=\{e,g_{r}|g_{r}^{\tiny 2}=e\}
. Consequently, the
PMD

\pmd(x,y)
is
\mathbb{C}_{2}
-invariant.

This paper tackles the problem of estimating the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}\,{=}\,\cdot]
, and, more generally, conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}})
, for random variables
{\mathbf{x}}\,{\in}\,{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}\,{\in}\,{\mathcal{Y}}
, under the assumption that
2
2

2

Throughout, with some abuse of notation we denote by
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}})
and
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}})
both the probability and conditional probability, respectively, as well as the corresponding densities, when they exist.

\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}})
and
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}})
are
\G
-invariant under
known
symmetry transformations of the data (as depicted in
Figure

3
), i.e:

\small\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}})\!=\!\mathbb{P}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\mathbf{y}}|g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathbf{x}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}})=\mathbb{P}(g\!\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathbf{x}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\;g\in\G,

(6)

where
\G
denotes a finite symmetry group (
Definition

H.1
) acting on the data spaces
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
via the group actions,
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}:\G\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}},
and
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}:\G\times{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, with
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}
denoting linear, invertible transformations of
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
defined by
g\in\G
(see
Figures

3
and
H.2
).

These priors imply the
\G
-invariance of the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
and of
{\mathbf{y}}
’s marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}})
, as well as the
\G
-equivariance of conditional expectations (see
Figure

3
-middle and
Proposition

C.1
):

\small g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}]{=}\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}]\hskip 9.24994pt\;\forall\;g\in\G,{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}.

(7)

Note that (
7
) implies the
\G
-equivariance of the regression function

{\bm{x}}\mapsto\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}\,{=}\,{\bm{x}}]
(see
Figure

3
-middle).
Therefore, the symmetry priors
of (
6
) are satisfied whenever we approximate an equivariant/invariant function—that is, in virtually
all applications of
GDL

(
Bronstein et al., 2021
)
.

The above symmetry priors represent a strong inductive bias for the conditional expectation operator (
2
), as they lead the
PMD
kernel defining the operator to be
\G
-invariant (see
Figure

3
-right):

\small\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=\pmd(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\;g\in\G,{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}},{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}.

(8)

In
Section

4
, we extend the
NCP
framework
(
Kostic et al., 2024a
)
by leveraging (
8
) to incorporate symmetry priors. This enables efficient estimation of
\G
-invariant conditional probabilities (
6
) and
\G
-equivariant regression (
7
) using
GDL
architectures, via (
2
), with strong learning guarantees.

4
ENCP Method for Equivariant Representation Learning

In this section, we show how to incorporate the symmetry priors (
6
) into
NCP
’s representation learning framework. First, we analyze the symmetry constraints on the infinite-dimensional conditional expectation operator and prove that, for symmetric random variables
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
, the optimal solution of (
3
) yields
\G
-equivariant representations
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}
and
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}
and approximates the operator with a
\G
-equivariant matrix
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
. Then, we explain how to embed these structural constraints into the bilinear neural network architecture of
NCP
using
any
type of equivariant
NNs
.

Symmetric Function Spaces

The assumption of
\G
-invariance of the marginal probabilities (
Section

3
) implies that the function spaces

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}

are symmetric Hilbert spaces of
\G
-equivariant functions, as these inherit unitary group actions

\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}$}}}}\colon\G\times\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}\to\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
and

\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}$}}}}\colon\G\times\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}\to\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}

defined via the push-forward of symmetry transformations of the data spaces (see details in
Appendix

I
and in
Figure

21
):

\displaystyle g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}}$}}}}f(\cdot)

\displaystyle:=f(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}\cdot)\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{},\;\;\text{ and }

(9)

\displaystyle g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}}$}}}}h(\cdot):=h(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}\cdot)\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{},\hskip 9.24994pt\forall g\in\G.

A fundamental property of
\G
-symmetric Hilbert spaces is their orthogonal decomposition into
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}\leq|\G|
subspaces referred to as
isotypic subspaces
:

\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}{=}\bm{\oplus}^{\perp}_{{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
, and

\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}{=}\bm{\oplus}^{\perp}_{{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
(see
Theorem

H.17
).
Where each

\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and

\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}

denote the spaces of
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant functions of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
, with
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}:=\G/{\mathbb{N}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}
being a
quotient subgroup
, generated by a normal subgroup
{\mathbb{N}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}
defined by the kernel of the group’s
{k}^{\text{th}}
irreducible representation (see
Section

H.2
).

This standard result from harmonic analysis
(
Mackey, 1980
)
enables us to express any
\G
-equivariant function as a sum of its projections onto the isotypic subspaces:

\displaystyle f(\cdot)=

\displaystyle f^{{\text{inv}}}(\cdot)+\sum_{{k}=2}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}f^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}(\cdot),\hskip 9.24994pth(\cdot)=h^{{\text{inv}}}(\cdot)+\sum_{{k}=2}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}h^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}(\cdot),

(10)

\displaystyle\text{with }\;f^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}{\in}\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{},h^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}{\in}\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{},\forall\;{k}\in[{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}],

where
f^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
and
h^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
denote the
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant components of
f
and
h
. Moreover, by convention, we associate the first subspace (
{k}=1
) with the space of
\G
-invariant functions, i.e.,
\G^{(1)}{=}\G^{{\text{inv}}}{=}\{e\}
(see
Example

I.4
in the Appendix).

Equivariant Conditional Expectation Operator

The
\G
-invariance of the
PMD
kernel (
8
), implies that
\CE
is a
\G
-equivariant linear operator (see
Definition

J.1
). This means that
\CE
commutes with the group action on the function spaces, and consequently due to Shur’s lemma (
Lemma

H.10
), can be decomposed (disentangled) into a direct sum of operators acting on the corresponding isotypic subspaces (see details in
Appendix

J
), that is:

\displaystyle g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}}$}}}}[\CE h](\cdot)=

\displaystyle\CE[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}}$}}}}h](\cdot)\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\;h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{},g\in\G,\;\;\text{ gives }

(11)

\displaystyle[\CE h](\cdot)=\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}[\CE^{\scalebox{1.0}{${{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}$}}h^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}](\cdot)

where each
\CE^{\scalebox{1.0}{${{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}$}}\colon\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}\to\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
models the conditional expectation for
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant functions.

Equivariant Representation Learning

The
\G
-equivariant structure of
\CE
and its disentanglement (
11
) into isotypic components suggests that computing the conditional expectation of a
\G
-equivariant function is equivalent to summing the conditional expectations of its
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant components for all
{k}\in[{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]
. Therefore, the loss function of problem (
3
), where
\CE
is approximated in finite dimensional spaces
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
and
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}
, decouples into
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
independent (disentangled) components:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \argmin_{\nnParams}\;\|\CE{-}{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}&=\textstyle{\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}\|\CE^{\scalebox{1.0}{${{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}$}}{-}{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}^{\scalebox{1.0}{${{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}$}}\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\textstyle{\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}(\pmd^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}){-}\pmd^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}))^{2},\end{aligned}

(12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{s.t. }\;&\pmd_{\nnParams}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})=\pmd_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}),\\
&\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})=1,\;\;\forall g\in\G,({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}.\end{aligned}

Importantly, to satisfy the
\G
-invariance constraint on
\pmd_{\nnParams}
, the truncated operator
{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\colon\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}{\to}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
must act on symmetric finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces that are stable under
\G
, i.e.,
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}$}}}}f\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
and
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}$}}}}h\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}
for all
f\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
and
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}
, and have a
\G
-equivariant matrix representation
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
(see
Proposition

J.2
). Thus, as in the infinite-dimensional case, these finite-dimensional spaces decompose into
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
isotypic subspaces

\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}{=}\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}

and

\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}{=}\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}
. Accordingly, the truncated operator matrix decomposes block-diagonally into
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
blocks, i.e.,
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}^{\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}
, where each
{k}
-th block needs to be a
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant matrix approximating the restriction of the conditional expectation operator on its corresponding isotypic subspace
(
Salova et al., 2019
;
Ordoñez-Apraez et al., 2024
)
, see
Figure

2
-right.

Analogous to (
4
), the optimal truncation of
\CE^{\scalebox{1.0}{${{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}$}}
is given by its truncated
SVD

(
Weidmann, 2012
)
. However, the
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariance of each disentangled component imposes additional structure on this
SVD
: any symmetry-transformed singular function remains a singular function in the same singular space
(
Ordoñez-Apraez et al., 2024
)
. Consequently, each singular space must have dimension at least
d_{{k}}
—the smallest real vector-space dimension in which
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
can be faithfully represented, that is, the dimension of the irreducible representation of type
{k}
; see
Figure

2
-right and
Proposition

J.3
.

Equivariant
NN
Parametrization

To solve
\G
-equivariant regression and estimate
\G
-invariant conditional probabilities via (
12
), we propose the disentangled bilinear
NN
setup in
Figure

2
-right. This approach supports any
GDL

\G
-equivariant backbone (e.g.,
MLP
,
CNN
,
GNN
, Transformer), adapting to diverse data modalities and tasks. Our framework can handle
continuous
compact groups via group discretization, and
non-compact
groups by selecting appropriate backbones, as it is done with
\G
-steerable
CNNs
for image/audio processing with
non-compact translation
equivariance (see details in
Section

D.1
and
Cesa et al. (2022)
).

The representation functions
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}:{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
can be parameterized by
any

\G
-equivariant
NN
architecture, with an additional non-learnable linear output layer that applies a change of basis to the isotypic basis, i.e.,

\small\begin{split}\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}(\cdot)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{\text{inv}}(\cdot)\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{({n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}})}(\cdot)\end{bmatrix}={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\top}({\bm{f}}_{\nnParams}(\cdot)-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{x}}[{\bm{f}}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}})])\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\\
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}(\cdot)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{\text{inv}}(\cdot)\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{({n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}})}(\cdot)\end{bmatrix}={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}^{\top}({\bm{h}}_{\nnParams}(\cdot)-\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{y}}[{\bm{h}}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})]).\end{split}

Here,
{\bm{f}}_{\nnParams}:{\mathcal{X}}\to\R^{r}
and
{\bm{h}}_{\nnParams}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\R^{r}
denote any
\G
-equivariant backbone architectures that output
r
-dimensional representations of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
. These representations are then centered to satisfy the centering constraint in (
12
). Moreover,
{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
and
{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}
are orthogonal, non-learnable change-of-basis matrices that expose the isotypic subspaces of
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
and
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}
(see details in
Remark

H.18
).

Such an orthogonal decomposition of the learned equivariant representations is referred to in the representation learning literature as a
disentangled
representation
(
Higgins et al., 2018
)
(see
Definition

H.19
). In our setting, disentanglement means that the representation decomposes into orthogonal components, each associated with a distinct group of symmetry transformations. Specifically, each component
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to\R^{r_{k}}
and
\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to\R^{r_{k}}
spans the approximated isotypic subspace of
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant functions, i.e.,

\small\begin{split}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}=\bm{\oplus}^{\perp}_{{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with }\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}:=\operatorname{span}(\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}})\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\\
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}=\bm{\oplus}^{\perp}_{{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with }\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}:=\operatorname{span}(\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}})\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}.\end{split}

Furthermore, the truncated operator’s matrix is parameterized in block-diagonal form

{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}^{\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}
,
with each block an
r_{{k}}{\times}r_{{k}}

\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant matrix
3
3

3

We chose square matrices for notational convenience. Dimensions for
{\mathbf{y}}
and
{\mathbf{x}}
spaces need not match
. The corresponding approximated
\G
-invariant
PMD
kernel is given by:

\displaystyle\pmd_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\mux}({\bm{x}})\mathds{1}_{\muy}({\bm{y}})+\textstyle{\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}),\;\;\;\text{ with }

(13)

\displaystyle\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}):=\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{x}})^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}^{\scalebox{1.0}{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}}\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{y}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]

where
\mathds{1}_{\mux}({\bm{x}})\mathds{1}_{\muy}({\bm{y}})
arises since the first singular functions of
\CE
are constant, similarly as in (
4
).

This parameterization inherently guarantees that the learned truncated operator
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
satisfies the
\G
-equivariance constraint and that the learned representation spaces satisfy the symmetry constraints imposed on the singular spaces of each
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
isotypic subspace. These structural constraints enable sharper symmetry-aware statistical learning guarantees in theory and improved empirical performance in practice; see
Sections

5
,
J.2
and
6
.

Disentangled Training Loss

Having introduced the equivariance constraints on the truncated operator matrix, we decompose the contrastive loss (
5
) to reflect the separability of the optimization arising from the operator’s isotypic decomposition (
11
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\nnParams)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}{-}2\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})+\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})^{2}+

(14)

\displaystyle\gamma\Omega^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}(\nnParams)+2\gamma\big(\|\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}^{\text{inv}}({\mathbf{x}})\|_{F}^{2}{+}\|\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}^{\text{inv}}({\mathbf{y}})\|_{F}^{2}\big).

This decomposes learning
\G
-equivariant representations of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
into learning
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
less constrained
\G^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
-equivariant representations for distinct quotient groups of
\G
.

Task

{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}}):=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}]\approx\hat{{\bm{f}}}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}})

\mathbb{P}[{\mathbf{y}}{\in}{\mathbb{B}}|{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}]\approx\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\nnParams}[{\mathbf{y}}\!\in\!{\mathbb{B}}|{\mathbf{x}}\!\in\!{\mathbb{A}}]

Estimate

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[{\mathbf{y}}]{+}\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}})^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})\otimes{\mathbf{y}}]

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{B}}]{+}\frac{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{x}})\otimes\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}})]^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{B}}({\mathbf{y}})\otimes\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})]}{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{A}}({\mathbf{x}})]}

Guarantees

\|{\bm{f}}{-}\hat{{\bm{f}}}_{\nnParams}\|_{\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}}{\lesssim}\scalebox{0.7}{$\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\left\|{\mathbf{y}}\right\|]}$}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}{+}\tfrac{\ln(\nicefrac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{{\delta}})}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}N)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+2\alpha}}}\right)

|\mathbb{P}{-}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\nnParams}|{\lesssim}\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathbb{P}}[{\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathbb{B}}]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}[{\mathbf{x}}\in\G\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.5}{${\mathcal{X}}$}$}}}}{\mathbb{A}}]}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}{+}\tfrac{\ln(\nicefrac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{{\delta}})}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}N)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+2\alpha}}}\right)

Table 1
:

Statistical guarantees for
eNCP
. The error bounds are governed by three factors: (i) the estimation error, impacted by the structure of the symmetry group
\G
—specifically, the sum of the minimum dimensionalities of the singular subspaces associated with each isotypic component,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}=\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}d_{{k}}}
, equivalently the sum of the dimensions of the irreducible representations of the group (see
Figure

2
), which increases the effective sample size; (ii) the representation learning error, measuring the quality of the learned representations, quantified by
\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}=\|\CE-{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\sigma_{r}}}{{\sigma_{r}-\sigma_{r+1}}}}\sqrt{\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\nnParams)-\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\star)}
; and (iii) the singular-value decay rate
\alpha>0
of the operator. Here,
\G\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathbb{A}}:=\cup_{g\in\G}\;g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathbb{A}}
denotes the group orbit of
{\mathbb{A}}
(
Definition

H.3
).

Moreover, we improve the estimates of the regularization terms in (
5
) by leveraging our symmetry priors to: (i) tighten the centering regularization (
14
) given that functions in
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
and
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
are centered by construction for
{k}\neq{\text{inv}}
(see
Corollary

K.4
)—and (ii) exploit the orthogonality between isotypic subspaces (
10
) to independently regularize orthonormality for each isotypic subspace (see example in
Figure

11
), leading to better covariance estimates
(
Shah & Chandrasekaran, 2012
)
:

\small\Omega^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}(\nnParams)\!:=\!\textstyle{\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}\|\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{\phi}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}})\!-\!{\bm{I}}_{r_{k}}\|^{2}_{F}\!+\!\|\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{\psi}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}})\!-\!{\bm{I}}_{r_{k}}\|^{2}_{F}.

(15)

Given a batch
\{({\bm{x}}_{n},{\bm{y}}_{n})\}_{n=1}^{N}
and their corresponding embeddings
\{(\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}}_{n}),\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{y}}_{n}))\}_{n=1}^{N}
, the empirical unregularized loss is estimated via U-statistics, yielding an unbiased estimate with an effective sample size of
N^{2}

(
Wang et al., 2022b
;
Tsai et al., 2020
)
.

\small\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}(\nnParams)\!=\!\sum_{{k}\in[{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}\Bigg[\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{n\in[N]}\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{x}}_{n},{\bm{y}}_{n})+\\
\tfrac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{a\in[N]}\sum_{b\in[N]\setminus\{a\}}\pmd_{\nnParams}^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}({\bm{x}}_{a},{\bm{y}}_{b})^{2}\Bigg].\end{split}

(16)

Similarly, we use U-statistics to obtain unbiased estimates for orthonormal regularization in (
15
), achieving an effective sample size of
d_{{k}}N^{2}
per isotypic subspace (see
Section

E.2
). Consequently, standard
NN
optimization methods can be employed to learn equivariant representations via the approximate model of
\CE
, enabling downstream inference tasks described in the next section.

5
Inference and Learning Guarantees

Once training is complete, the learned
\G
-invariant
PMD
from (
13
) can be used, via (
2
), for
\G
-equivariant regression and
\G
-invariant conditional probability estimation. These estimates are obtained using a
NN
architecture composed of
\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}
,
{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
, and a final linear layer that delivers the basis expansion coefficients of the target variable in the
{\mathbf{y}}
representation space
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}=\operatorname{span}(\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams})
. For a summary of the estimates and their learning guarantees refer to
Table

1
.

Both estimates are derived from the general problem of vector-valued regression of a target function
{\bm{z}}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
defined by the conditional expectation of an observable
{\bm{h}}\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\muy}$}{({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{Z}})}
, that is,
{\bm{z}}({\bm{x}}):=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}={\bm{x}}]=[\CE{\bm{h}}]({\bm{x}})
, where
{\mathcal{Z}}
is a symmetric vector space. Using the learned model, we estimate
{\bm{z}}({\bm{x}}):=[\CE{\bm{h}}]({\bm{x}})
by:

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}}):=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})]+\bm{\phi}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}})^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\,\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})\otimes{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})],

(17)

where
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[\bm{\psi}_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{y}})\otimes{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})]
represents the basis expansion coefficients of
{\bm{h}}
in the learned basis of
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{y}}$}}\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
. Here,
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}\to\R
and
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}\colon\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}\to\R
are the
\G
-invariant empirical expectations defined by:

\small\begin{split}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[f({\mathbf{a}})]\!=\!\frac{1}{|\G|N}\sum_{g\in\G}\sum_{n{=}1}^{N}f(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{A}}$}}}}\!{\bm{a}}_{n})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }{\bm{a}}\in\{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\}\end{split}

(18)

Hence, our method learns representations of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
that transform
nonlinear regression of observables
into
linear regression
in the learned representation space. For example, assuming
{\mathbf{y}}
has bounded variance and setting
{\bm{h}}({\bm{y}})={\bm{y}}
, we recover the standard (
\G
-equivariant) regression solution (see
Table

1
-left). Equally important, by letting
{\bm{h}}=\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}
—the indicator of a measurable set
{\mathbb{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}
—the model estimates conditional probabilities (see
Table

1
-right), thereby supporting both regression and uncertainty quantification (e.g., conditional quantiles, see
Section

6
and
Kostic et al. (2024b)
). The following learning bounds cover this general setting.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let the symmetry priors in (
6
) hold,
\CE
be a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}
-Schatten-class
\G
-equivariant conditional expectation operator, as in (
11
), and
{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}
be a truncated approximation, defined in (
13
) and trained via (
14
), with rank
r
and parameters
\nnParams\in\Theta
.
Then, given an appropriate truncation dimension
r\asymp(\nicefrac{{N}}{{d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}^{\alpha}}})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{(1+2\alpha)}}}
, the following results hold for any
\G
-equivariant/invariant
{\bm{h}}\!\in\!\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\muy}$}{({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{Z}})}
, measurable set
{\mathbb{A}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\G^{\prime}\leq\G
; with probability at least
1-\delta
w.r.t. an iid draw of
{\mathbb{D}}_{N}=\{({\mathbf{x}}_{n},{\mathbf{y}}_{n})\sim\muxy\}_{n=1}^{N}
:

\small\|{\bm{z}}-\hat{{\bm{z}}}_{\nnParams}\|_{\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\mux}$}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Z}})}\!\lesssim\!\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\left\|{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})\right\|_{{\mathcal{Z}}}]}\;\cdot\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\xi_{\nnParams}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}

(19a)

\small\begin{split}\|{\bm{z}}({\mathbb{A}})-\hat{{\bm{z}}}_{\nnParams}({\mathbb{A}})\|_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\!\lesssim\!\tfrac{\sqrt{1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{(|\G^{\prime}|\,{-}\,1)\gamma_{\G^{\prime}}({\mathbb{A}})}}}\,\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\left\|{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})\right\|_{{\mathcal{Z}}}]}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sqrt{|\G^{\prime}|\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}})}}}\;\cdot\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\xi_{\nnParams}}\end{split}

(19b)

where the modeling/approximation and finite-sample-estimation error are given by:

\small{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\xi_{\nnParams}}:=\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}+\frac{\ln(\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}}{{\delta}})}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}N)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+2\alpha}}}.

Here,
\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}=\|\CE{-}{\mathsf{E}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nnParams$}}\|_{\text{op}}
denotes representation learning modeling error (
12
),
r=\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}d_{{k}}m_{k}
defines the representation space dimension, with
(d_{{k}},m_{k})
denoting dimension and multiplicity of the group’s irreducible representation of type
{k}
, and
d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}:=\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}d_{{k}}\geq{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
(see
Figure

2
).

Proof.

\G
-invariance of
\mux
and
\muy
allows us to control both bias (
Theorem

L.2
) and variance (
Proposition

L.3
) of
\hat{{\bm{z}}}_{\nnParams}
. A simple balancing of
r
yields the final bound on the error.
∎

We conclude by highlighting key theoretical and practical implications of
Theorem

5.1
. Since pointwise guarantees are essential in many applications,
Equation

19b
provides a learning bound conditioned on measurable sets
{\mathbb{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, yielding the estimate

{\bm{z}}({\mathbb{A}}):=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{y}}}[{\bm{h}}({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}]\approx\nicefrac{{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}[\widehat{{\bm{z}}}_{\nnParams}({\bm{x}})]}}{{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}[\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}}({\mathbf{x}})]}}
. In this setting, exploiting symmetry helps mitigate the bottlenecks of rare-event estimation. To capture this effect, we introduce the
symmetry index
of
{\mathbb{A}}
w.r.t.
\mux
, which quantifies the degree of symmetry of
{\mathbb{A}}
and appears in (
19b
):

\small\begin{split}\gamma_{\G}({\mathbb{A}})=\frac{1}{|\G|-1}{\sum_{g\in\G\setminus\{e\}}}\frac{\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}\cap\;g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.8}{${\mathcal{X}}$}$}}}}{\mathbb{A}})}{\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}})},\\
\end{split}

(20)

Observe that
\gamma_{\G}({\mathbb{A}})\in[0,1]
for any
{\mathbb{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
, with

\gamma_{\G}({\mathbb{A}})=1
if
{\mathbb{A}}
is
\G
‑invariant (e.g., the vertical and horizontal reflection planes in
Figure

3
), while
\gamma_{\G}({\mathbb{A}})=0
if
{\mathbb{A}}
is a
\G
‑asymmetric set, that is, if
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathbb{A}}\cap{\mathbb{A}}=\emptyset
for all
g\in\G
(e.g., any set disjoint from the reflection planes in
Figure

3
). In particular, the effective rarity of
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}
is captured by
\gamma_{\G^{\prime}}({\mathbb{A}})
, yielding a maximal gain of
|\G|\mathbb{P}[{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}]\gg\mathbb{P}[{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}]
when
{\mathbb{A}}
is asymmetric.

Equivariant disentangled representations increase the effective sample size according to

N\ll{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}N\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}N}\leq|\G|N
.
Thus, they provide not only the expected
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
-fold gain from disentanglement, but also the stronger boost induced by

d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}=\sum_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}d_{{k}}
(see
Figure

2
, right). This improvement in the estimation-error term of the bound is achieved by exploiting the structural constraints of the singular spaces associated with the irreducible representations of the group that appear in the chosen representation space. Moreover, any non-trivial symmetry group sharpens the bound. In the absence of a symmetry prior, namely when
\G=\{e\}
and
|\G|={n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}=d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}=1
, our framework recovers the symmetry-agnostic baseline results of
(
Kostic et al., 2024b
)
.

The remaining term in the bound is the representation-learning error
\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}
inherited from
NCP

(
Kostic et al., 2024b
)
. We do not provide a full statistical characterization of this term, as doing so would require additional architecture- and regularity-dependent assumptions without changing the main qualitative conclusions. Instead, we complement the theory with synthetic experiments in which the true operator is known, allowing us to empirically assess the impact of exploiting symmetry on
\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}
(see
Figures

14
and
6
).

Figure 4
:

Sample-efficiency plots for the test-set
PMD

MSE

\pmd_{\text{mse}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}}(\pmd({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}))^{2}

versus the number of training samples, on logarithmic scales.
Each column corresponds to a symmetric
cGMM
with a different symmetry group and
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
dimensionality. The tested groups are the cyclic groups
\mathbb{C}_{2}
and
\mathbb{C}_{6}
, the dihedral group
\mathbb{D}_{6}
, and the icosahedral group
{\mathbb{I}}_{h}
(order 60).

Finally, the parameter
\alpha
quantifies problem regularity through the decay rate of the operator’s singular values,
\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{i}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}<\infty
, with special cases including finite-rank operators (
\alpha=\infty
), compact operators (
\alpha=0
), trace class operators (
\alpha=1
), and Hilbert-Schmidt operators (
\alpha=1/2
, equivalent to
\pmd\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\mux\times\muy}$}({\mathcal{X}}\!\times\!{\mathcal{Y}})
; see
Appendix

L
).
Thus, our results cover learning rates ranging from arbitrarily slow rates as
\alpha\to 0
to fast rates
[d_{{\text{\rm iso}}}N]^{-1/2}
as
\alpha\to\infty
.

6
Experiments

We present three experiments evaluating our method in (i) approximating the conditional expectation operator and the use of the learned operator for (ii)
\G
-equivariant regression and (iii) symmetry-aware uncertainty quantification. For further details and additional experiments on synthetic regression, uncertainty quantification, symmetry misspecification and train/inference computational costs refer to
Appendix

F
and the code repository
1

Conditional Expectation Operator Learning

This experiment quantifies the
Mean Squared Error (MSE)
of approximating
\CE
, i.e.,
\pmd_{\text{mse}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}}(\pmd({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})-\pmd_{\nnParams}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}))^{2}
. To achieve this, we extend the
Conditional Gaussian Mixture Model (cGMM)
of
Gilardi et al. (2002)
to parametrically construct symmetric vector-valued random variables
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}
that satisfy the symmetry priors in (
6
) for arbitrary finite symmetry groups (see example in
Figure

3
). Each
cGMM
possess an analytical
PMD
, enabling direct
\pmd_{\text{mse}}
estimation, usually impossible for real-world datasets.

The results in
Figure

4
compare our
eNCP
against
NCP

(
Kostic et al., 2024b
)
, the baseline
Density Ratio Fitting (DRF)

(
Tsai et al., 2020
)
, and our
Invariant Density Ratio Fitting (iDRF)
adaptation. Note that the baselines approximate
\pmd
as a single
NN
,
\pmd_{\nnParams}^{\text{drf}}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\to\R^{+}
, trained via the contrastive loss in (
5
). Since they do not enforce the separable structure in (
13
), these methods cannot be used for regression or conditional probability estimation.

We evaluate performance across
cGMMs
with diverse symmetry groups and varying
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
dimensions. Across all settings,
eNCP
achieves lower
MSE
and better sample efficiency than
iDRF
and the symmetry-agnostic models
NCP
and
DRF
(see
Figure

4
). Moreover,
Fig.

6
shows that the symmetry-agnostic models (
NCP
and
DRF
) struggle to recover the
\G
-invariance of the true
PMD
from data alone. In contrast,
eNCP
and
iDRF
explicitly enforce the invariance constraint in
Equation

12
through architectural
NN
constraints, preserving the desired
\G
-invariance throughout training up to numerical precision. These results show that
eNCP
accurately approximates the conditional expectation operator, consistently achieving lower empirical representation-learning errors
\mathcal{E}_{\nnParams}^{r}
than all other models. This underscores the critical role of symmetry in improving both accuracy and sample efficiency.

\G
-Equivariant Regression

To test our model’s potential for performing
\G
-equivariant regression, we address the robot’s
Center of Mass (CoM)
momenta regression task of
(
Ordoñez-Apraez et al., 2025
)
. The goal is to predict a quadruped robot’s
CoM
linear
{\bm{l}}\in\R^{3}
and angular momenta
{\bm{k}}\in\R^{3}
given the noisy observations of the robot’s generalized positions
{\bm{q}}\in\R^{12}
and velocity coordinates
\dot{{\bm{q}}}\in\R^{12}
, i.e.,

[{\bm{l}}^{\top},{\bm{k}}^{\top}]^{\top}=h_{\text{CoM}}({\bm{q}}+\epsilon_{{\bm{q}}},\dot{{\bm{q}}}+\epsilon_{\dot{{\bm{q}}}})

(see details in
Sections

F.2
and
7
). We compare
eNCP
against
NCP
and two baselines—a standard
Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP)
and an
Equivariant MLP (eMLP)
—all with equivalent architectural footprint. The
NCP
and
eNCP
are trained using (
5
) and (
14
), respectively, while
MLP
and
eMLP
are trained using standard
MSE
.

Figure 5
:

Prediction of 90%
CIs
(light red areas) for ground-reaction forces
{\bm{\tau}}_{\text{grf}}\in\R^{12}
of a quadruped robot on rough terrain with varying friction. We compare
eNCP
,
NCP
,
eCQR
, and
CQR
models. CIs are computed along
x
,
y
,
z
compoents of the forces on the front-right (FR) and front-left (FL) legs (see all legs predictions in
Figure

19
). The trajectory of ground reaction forces in time is shown in blue lines, while sensed values outside of the predicted
CIs
are plotted in red markers.

The results in
Figure

1
demonstrate that our
eNCP
model outperforms all other baselines in both performance and sample complexity. Consistent with
(
Kostic et al., 2024b
)
, the
NCP
model shows poorer sample complexity than
MLP
and
eMLP
due to its indirect approach to regression, via approximation of
\CE
. However, by incorporating symmetry priors,
eNCP
appear to mitigate this limitation.

Symmetry-Aware Uncertainty Quantification

Finally, we demonstrate the practical impact of our approach on a core robotics problem: providing robust uncertainty quantification for unavailable yet crucial state observables for robot control and state estimation
(
Bledt et al., 2018
;
Maravgakis et al., 2023
)
. Specifically, we use proprioceptive sensor readings to provide
90\%

CIs
for the robot’s
Ground Reaction Forces (GRF)

{\bm{\tau}}_{\text{grf}}\in\R^{12}
, the instantaneous work exerted or subtracted to the robot
U({\bm{q}},\dot{{\bm{q}}},{\bm{\tau}})\in\R
, and the kinetic energy
T({\bm{q}},\dot{{\bm{q}}})\in\R
, while the robot traverses rough terrain (see
Section

F.6
). Reliable probabilistic estimates of these quantities are of crucial relevance for optimal control
(
Bledt et al., 2018
)
, contact detection
(
Maravgakis et al., 2023
)
, state estimation
(
Nisticò et al., 2025
)
, and system identification
(
Gautier, 1997
)
.

r-Coverage
\uparrow

Coverage
\uparrow

Set-size
\downarrow

eNCP

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}99.5{\pm}0.1\%}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}95.0{\pm}0.4\%}

4.3{\pm}3.6{\times}10^{9}

NCP

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}99.5{\pm}0.0\%}

56.9{\pm}0.3\%

2.6{\pm}1.4{\times}10^{10}

eCQR

84.2{\pm}0.7\%

6.7{\pm}1.2\%

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1.7{\pm}1.7{\times}10^{7}}

Table 2:

Relaxed coverage (
29
), coverage (
28
), and set-size (
30
) for predicted
CIs
on the test set of the quadruped locomotion uncertainty-estimation task with
{\mathbf{y}}=[{\bm{\tau}}_{\text{grf}}^{\top},\,U,\,T]^{\top}
. The target coverage is
90\%
. See all metrics in
Table

4
.

This task tests our model’s ability to learn conditional distributions from high-dimensional data, considering that for the
eNCP
and
NCP
models, quantile estimation is done by regressing the
conditional Cumulative Distribution Function (cCDF)
for each dimension of
{\mathbf{y}}=[{\textnormal{y}}_{1},\dots]
and then applying a linear search to extract quantiles (see
Figures

10
and
14
). This is achieved by discretizing the range of each
{\textnormal{y}}_{i}
into
N_{b}
bins and estimating

\mathbb{P}({\textnormal{y}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{i,n}|{\mathbf{x}}=\cdot):=[\CE\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}_{i,n}}](\cdot)

for all
n\in[N_{b}]
(see
Section

5
), where
{\mathbb{A}}_{i,n}
consists of the first
n
bins. In practice, this means regressing
|{\mathcal{Y}}|\times N_{b}
conditional probabilities corresponding to sets of varying sizes in a
single forward pass
(see details in
Section

F.4
). By contrast, the baseline
Conditional Quantile Regression (CQR)

(
Feldman et al., 2023
)
and its equivariant adaptation
Equivariant CQR (eCQR)
directly regress quantiles for a fixed coverage level (i.e., the probability that an event lies within the predicted confidence interval) and need retraining for different coverage values.

The results, shown in
Tables

2
and
1
for work
U
and kinetic energy
T
, and in
Figure

5
for the ground reaction forces
{\bm{\tau}}_{\text{grf}}
, identify eNCP as the only model that provides robust uncertainty quantification under both transient disturbances and nominal locomotion conditions. It is also the only model whose empirical test-set coverage remains close to the target value, rendering the other models unreliable in practice. These results further highlight eNCP’s potential for conditional probability estimation.

7
Conclusions

We introduce a novel framework for equivariant contrastive representation learning that enables equivariant regression, symmetry-aware uncertainty quantification, and conditional probability estimation with non-asymptotic statistical learning guarantees. Building on a recent contrastive representation learning method for approximating the spectral decomposition of the conditional expectation operator, our approach incorporates symmetry priors to impose additional structural constraints on the operator approximation, yielding a disentangled representation that admits stronger symmetry-aware statistical guarantees. We demonstrate the framework’s benefits through theoretical learning bounds and empirical evaluations on robotics applications. Notably, we provide the first learning guarantees for equivariant regression and uncertainty quantification using neural network features, bridging spectral representation learning and geometric deep learning.

Limitations and Future Work

Our method relies on fully specified symmetry priors, a natural assumption in
GDL
; however, some applications may have only partial or misspecified symmetries. Future work could accommodate partial or uncertain symmetry information and statistically test for its presence in data, as explored in
Section

F.5
.

Impact Statement

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field
of Machine Learning. There are many potential societal
consequences of our work, none which we feel must be
specifically highlighted here.

The appendix is organized as follows.

•

Appendix

A
summarizes the notations used, while
Part

provides a glossary.

•

Appendix

B
offers a detailed discussion of related work on contrastive learning, equivariant representations, and statistical learning theory with symmetry priors.

•

Appendix

C
-
Appendix

F
detail our methodological contributions. In particular,
Appendix

D
and
Appendix

E
explain how our method leverages equivariant neural networks, and
Appendix

F
complements
Section

6
with additional experimental details and studies.

•

We provide self-contained sections with unified notation covering essential preliminaries: group theory (
Appendix

H
), representation theory in function spaces (
Appendix

I
), equivariant linear operators (
Appendix

J
), and the symmetries of covariance operators central to our work (
Appendix

K
).

•

Finally,
Appendix

L
presents our theoretical contributions, summarized in Theorem
Theorem

5.1
. We first establish approximation error bounds using operator theory and equivariant representations, then combine group theory with concentration inequalities to derive estimation error bounds that fully expose the benefits of symmetry priors.
