.

Training Time Overhead

Tab.

5
shows that
eNCP
incurs a per-batch training overhead of roughly
3\times
–
6\times
in the forward pass and
2.2\times
–
4.1\times
in the backward pass. This overhead arises from the basis expansions in the equivariant layers (common to all equivariant
NN
implementations) and from the symmetry-aware estimation of means, covariances, and cross-covariances in the loss (
14
). Importantly, the computational overhead is governed by the number of isotypic subspaces rather than the order of the group, given that several algebraic operations are performed sequentially per each isotypic subspace.

Inference Time Overhead

At inference time, the equivariant
NN
can be run as a standard
NN
resulting in a minimal inference overhead
(
Ordonez Apraez, 2026
)
, around
1.1\times
–
1.2\times
across all groups. This mild increase is due to the additional change-of-basis layer used to expose the isotypic decomposition in the equivariant encoder (see
Remark

H.18
). Despite the per-batch overhead,
eNCP
converges substantially faster: as shown in
Figure

15
, it reaches a lower validation loss before epoch
500
than
NCP
achieves after
2500
epochs, corresponding to roughly
5\times
fewer training batches to reach a better solution. Combined with the
2\times
–
60\times
reduction in trainable parameters, this yields a favorable overall computational trade-off.

Appendix G
Conditional Probability Modeling via the Conditional Expectation Operator

This section introduces the modelling of conditional probabilities for two random variables via the
conditional expectation operator
. Our goal is to understand conditional expectation from an operator‑theoretic perspective. We begin by describing the marginal, joint, and conditional probabilities of the random variables within a measure‑theoretic framework. This discussion extends the exposition of
Kostic et al. (2024b)
.

Given two random variables
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
taking values in the measure spaces
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}},\mux)
and
({\mathcal{Y}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}},\muy)
, we have that the marginal probability of any set
{\mathbb{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathbb{B}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{Y}}
are given by

\small\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}})=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}}({\bm{x}})\mux(d{\bm{x}})=\int_{{\mathbb{A}}}\mux(d{\bm{x}})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathbb{B}})=\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}({\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}})=\int_{{\mathbb{B}}}\muy(d{\bm{y}}),

(31)

where
\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}}\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
and
\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
denote the characteristic functions of sets
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{B}}
, respectively.

Furthermore, under the reasonable assumption that the joint probability measure is absolutely continuous w.r.t to the product of the marginals
\muxy\ll\mux\times\muy
, we have that there exist a Radon-Nikodym derivative
\pmd:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\to\R_{+}
such that
\muxy(d{\bm{x}},d{\bm{y}})=\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mux(d{\bm{x}})\muy(d{\bm{y}})
. Note that
\pmd
is a kernel function that pointwise deforms the product of the marginals to produce the joint distribution
(
Sugiyama et al., 2012
)
(see
Figure

3
). This kernel function enable us to express the joint probability by:

\small\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathbb{B}}){=}\int_{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}}({\bm{x}})\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}({\bm{y}})\underbrace{\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}})\mux(d{\bm{x}})}_{\muxy(d{\bm{x}},d{\bm{y}})}{=}\int_{{\mathbb{A}}\times{\mathbb{B}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\mux(d{\bm{x}})\muy(d{\bm{y}}).

(32)

Furthermore, given that
\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}{\in}{\mathbb{B}}|{\mathbf{x}}{\in}{\mathbb{A}})=\nicefrac{{\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}{\in}{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbf{y}}{\in}{\mathbb{B}})}}{{\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}})}}
, the conditional probability of any set
{\mathbb{B}}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
given a value of the random variable
{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}
is given by:

\small\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{y}}{\in}{\mathbb{B}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}){=}\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}({\bm{y}}){P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}}(d{\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}}){=}\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}({\bm{y}})\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}})=\int_{{\mathbb{B}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}}),

(33)

where
{P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}}:\Sigma_{\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto[0,1]
denotes the
conditional probability measure
. This is a well-defined probability measure considering that:

\small\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}}):=\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbf{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}})=\int_{{\mathbb{A}}}\ \underbrace{\bigg(\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}})\bigg)}_{\mathbb{E}{P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}}(d{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}})=1\;\;\forall{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mux(d{\bm{x}})=\int_{{\mathbb{A}}}\mux(d{\bm{x}}).

The Operator Perspective

Every measurable function
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
can be approximated by simple functions—that is, as a combination of characteristic functions on measurable sets:
h(\cdot)\approx\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\beta_{i}\mathds{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}_{i}}(\cdot)
. Thus,
Equation

33
is a special case of the more general problem of approximating the conditional expectation of any function
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
given
{\mathbf{x}}
. This conditional expectation is captured by the action of a linear integral operator:

Definition G.1

(Conditional expectation operator)
.

Let
({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}})
be two random variables defined on the measure spaces
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}},\mux)
and
({\mathcal{Y}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}},\muy)
, respectively, and let

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}

denote the corresponding spaces of square-integrable functions. The conditional expectation operator
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}:\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}\to\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
is the linear integral operator—defined via the
PMD
Radon–Nikodym derivative

\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})=\nicefrac{{\muxy(d{\bm{x}},d{\bm{y}})}}{{\mux(d{\bm{x}})\,\muy(d{\bm{y}})}}

—which acts on any function
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
by computing its conditional expectation:

{\small[{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}h]({\bm{x}}){=}\mathbb{E}[h({\mathbf{y}})|{\mathbf{x}}{=}{\bm{x}}]{:=}\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}}){P_{{\mathbf{y}}|{\mathbf{x}}}}(d{\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}}){=}\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}})\tfrac{\muxy(d{\bm{y}},{\bm{x}})}{\mux(d{\bm{x}})}{=}\int_{{\mathcal{Y}}}h({\bm{y}})\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\muy(d{\bm{y}}).}

From a learning perspective, approximating the conditional expectation operator sufficiently well for a relevant set of functions in

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}

implies that we can approximate the conditional probability measure of any set
{\mathbb{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{Y}}
. This enables both regression
and
uncertainty quantification applications with a single model (see
Equation

2
).

Appendix H
Background on Group and Representation Theory

Group Actions and Representations

This section provides a concise overview of the fundamental concepts in group and representation theory, which are used to define the symmetries of the random variables we consider in this work. For a comprehensive background on these topics in finite-dimensional vector spaces, see
Weiler et al. (2023)
; for the infinite-dimensional case, consult
Knapp (1986)
. These concepts will be referenced as needed in the main text. To begin, we define a group as an abstract mathematical object.

Definition H.1

(Group)
.

A group is a set
\G
, endowed with a binary composition operator defined as:

\begin{matrix}(\mathrel{{\circ}}):&\G\times\G&\longrightarrow&\G\\
&(g_{1},g_{2})&\longrightarrow&g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{2},\end{matrix}

(34a)

such that the following axioms hold:

Associativity:

\displaystyle\quad(g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{2})\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{3}=g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}(g_{2}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{3}),\quad\forall\;g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in\G,

(34b)

Identity:

\displaystyle\quad\exists\;e\in\G\;\text{such that}\;e\mathrel{{\circ}}g=g=g\mathrel{{\circ}}e,\quad\forall\;g\in\G,

(34c)

Inverses:

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;g\in\G,\;\exists\;g^{-1}\in\G\;\text{such that}\;g\mathrel{{\circ}}g^{-1}=e=g^{-1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g.

(34d)

We are primarily interested in symmetry groups, i.e., groups of transformations acting on a set
{\mathcal{X}}
. Each transformation is a bijection that leaves a fundamental property of the element of the set invariant. For example, if
{\mathcal{X}}
represents states of a dynamical system, the invariant property is the state energy (see
Figure

7
); if
{\mathcal{X}}
is a data space, the preserved quantity is typically the probability density/distribution (see
Figure

3
).

Definition H.2

(Group action on a set
(
Weiler et al., 2023
)
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a set endowed with symmetry group
\G
. The (left) group action of the group
\G
on the set
{\mathcal{X}}
is a map:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}):&\G\times{\mathcal{X}}&\longrightarrow&{\mathcal{X}}\\
&(g,{\bm{x}})&\longrightarrow&g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}\end{matrix}

(35a)

that is compatible with the group composition and identity element
e\in\G
, in the sense that:

Identity:

\displaystyle\quad e\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}

(35b)

Associativity:

\displaystyle\quad(g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{2})\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}=g_{1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}(g_{2}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;g_{1},g_{2}\in\G,\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}.

(35c)

These sets of bijections describe structural properties of the set
{\mathcal{X}}
. To study these properties, we will frequently refer to the group of symmetry related elements of to a given element
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
as its
group orbit
of
{\bm{x}}
:

Definition H.3

(Group orbit)
.

Let
{\bm{x}}
be an element of the set
{\mathcal{X}}
endowed with symmetry group
\G
. The group orbit of
{\bm{x}}
is the the set of all symmetry related set elements, denoted by:

\G{\bm{x}}:=\{g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}\;|\;g\in\G\}\equiv\{{\bm{x}}\mathrel{{\triangleleft}}g\;|\;g\in\G\}.

For our practical purposes, we focus on symmetry transformations acting on sets with a vector space structure. In most cases, the group action on a vector space is linear, which allows us to express symmetry transformations as (orthogonal) matrix-vector operations, once a basis for the space is chosen.

Definition H.4

(Linear group representation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space endowed with symmetry group
\G
. A
linear representation
of
\G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
is a map, denoted by
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
, between symmetry transformation and invertible linear maps on
{\mathcal{X}}
(i.e., elements of the general linear group
\mathbb{GL}({\mathcal{X}})
):

\begin{matrix}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}:&\G&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{GL}({\mathcal{X}})\\
&g&\longrightarrow&\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g),\end{matrix}

(36a)

such that the following properties hold:

composition

\displaystyle:\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{2})=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g_{1})\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g_{2}),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;g_{1},g_{2}\in\G,

(36b)

inversion

\displaystyle:\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g^{-1})=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g)^{-1},\quad

\displaystyle\forall\;g\in\G.

(36c)

identity

\displaystyle:\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g\mathrel{{\circ}}g^{-1})=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(e)={\bm{I}},

(36d)

Whenever the vector space is of finite dimension
n<\infty
, linear maps admit a matrix form
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g)\in\R^{n\times n}
, once a basis set
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
for the vector space
{\mathcal{X}}
is chosen. In this case,
Equations

36b
,
36c
and
36d
show how the composition and inversion of symmetry transformations translate to matrix multiplication and inversion, respectively. Moreover,
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
allows to express a (linear) group action (
Definition

H.2
) as a matrix-vector multiplication:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}):&\G\times{\mathcal{X}}&\longrightarrow&{\mathcal{X}}\\
&(g,{\bm{x}})&\longrightarrow&g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}:=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{x}}.\end{matrix}

(36e)

We will often study linear maps that preserve a vector space’s symmetry structure by commuting with the group action. These maps are known as
endomorphisms
and include all change of basis and affine transformations that do not break the symmetry.

Definition H.5

(Endomorphism)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space endowed with symmetry group
\G
, with the group action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}:\G\times{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}
. A linear map
{\bm{A}}:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}
is said to be an endomorphism if it commutes with the group action, such that:

\vbox{\hbox{
$\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{A}}={\bm{A}}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g),\quad\forall\;g\in\G$
}}\qquad\iff\qquad\vbox{\hbox{
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.94444pt\raise 5.19017pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.31517pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\bm{A}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.5pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\bm{A}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.5pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.94444pt\raise-26.0876pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.31517pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}

We will denote the space of all endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{X}}
as
\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}})
, such that any
{\bm{A}}\in\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}})
satisfies the above commutation property.

So far, we have studied symmetric vector spaces
{\mathcal{X}}
endowed with a group action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}
, which can be represented in matrix form via a group representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
once a basis for
{\mathcal{X}}
is chosen. However, while the choice of basis alters the group representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
, the underlying group action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}
remains invariant. This observation leads us to the concept of equivalent group representations.

Definition H.6

(Equivalent group representations)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space endowed with symmetry group
\G
, and let
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
be two group representations of
\G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
. They are said to be
equivalent
, denoted by
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{\prime}\sim\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
, if there exists an invertible change of basis
{\bm{Q}}\in\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}})
such that

\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{\prime}(g)={\bm{Q}}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{Q}}^{-1},\quad\forall\;g\in\G.

(37)

Equivalent representations arise when the same group action
(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}):\G\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is expressed in different coordinate frames or bases. For instance, let
{\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
and
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
be two bases for
{\mathcal{X}}=\text{span}({\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})=\text{span}({\mathbb{B}}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
, and let
{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
denote the change of basis from
{\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
to
{\mathbb{B}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
, so that
{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{B}}={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}{\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{A}}}
for all
{\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{A}}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Then the group action admits equivalent representations,
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{\mathbb{A}}\sim\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{\mathbb{B}}
, since

\displaystyle g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle:={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{A}}),\qquad\forall g\in\G,

(38)

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}(g){\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}\bigl(\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(g){\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{A}}\bigr)=\Bigl({\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(g){{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}}^{-1}\Bigr){\bm{x}}^{\mathbb{B}},

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}(g)

\displaystyle={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(g){{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\mathbb{B}}}^{-1}.

To reveal the modular structure of symmetric vector spaces, we often change bases to decompose them into subspaces stable under the action of the group
\G
, termed
\G
-stable subspaces. This decomposition mirrors how a symmetry group can be broken down into products and direct products of smaller groups and is essential for analyzing and simplifying group representations. We introduce the following definition.

Definition H.7

(
\G
-stable and irreducible subspaces)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space endowed with a group action
(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}})
of the symmetry group
\G
. A subspace
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
is said to be
\G
-stable
if the action of any group element on any vector in the subspace remains within the subspace, that is,

g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime},\quad\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}},\forall\;g\in\G.

If the only
\G
-stable subspaces of
{\mathcal{X}}
are
\{\bm{0}\}
and
{\mathcal{X}}
itself, then
{\mathcal{X}}
is a
irreducible
\G
-stable space
. We will denote irreducible
\G
-stable spaces with an over bar, e.g.,
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}
.

Building Blocks of Symmetric Vector Spaces

A recurrent theme in this work and in geometric deep learning is to study and process symmetric vector spaces by decomposing them in terms of irreducible
\G
-stable spaces. This process directly corresponds to the decomposition of the associated group representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
into smaller representations acting on these
\G
-stable subspaces.

Definition H.8

(Decomposable representation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space with a group action
(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}})
defined by the representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
in a chosen basis
{\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
. The representation is
decomposable
if it is equivalent to a direct sum of two lower-dimensional representations,
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}\sim\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{1}$}}\oplus\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{2}$}}
, where
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
are
\G
-stable subspaces of
{\mathcal{X}}
. Equivalently, there exists a change of basis
{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
such that

\small\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{1}$}}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{2}$}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}{{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}}^{-1},\;\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptg\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}:=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}(g){\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{1}$}}(g){\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}_{1}\\
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{2}$}}(g){\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}_{2}\end{bmatrix},\;\text{where }{\bm{Q}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}{\bm{x}}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\\
{\bm{x}}^{{\mathbb{B}}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

Hence, the representation’s decomposition
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}\sim\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{1}$}}\oplus\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{2}$}}
corresponds to
decomposing the vector space
into
\G
-stable subspaces,
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
.

When iteratively applying the decomposition process, we eventually reach representations that cannot be further decomposed. These are known as irreducible representations, or
irreps
, and they serve as the fundamental building blocks for all representations of a compact symmetry group
\G
. From a vector space perspective, the irreducible
\G
-stable subspaces (
Definition

H.7
) associated with these irreps are the elementary subspaces that comprise any symmetric vector space, analogous to how one-dimensional subspaces are the fundamental components of standard vector spaces.

Definition H.9

(Irreducible representation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a vector space endowed with a group action
(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}})
of a symmetry group
\G
. A representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}
of
\G
on
{\mathcal{X}}
is said to be
irreducible
if it cannot be decomposed into smaller representations acting on proper
\G
-stable subspaces (
Definition

H.7
). That is, the only
\G
-stable subspaces
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
are
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}=\{\bm{0}\}
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{X}}
itself.

To differentiate irreps from decomposable representations we will denote the formers and their associated irreducible
\G
-stable spaces with an over bar:
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}$}}:\G\to\mathbb{GL}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
.

Crucially, any compact symmetry group
\G
has a unique set of countably many irreps, denoted by
\{\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}\}_{{k}\in[1,{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}]}
, where
{k}
denotes the irrep type and
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}\leq|\G|
denotes the number of unique irreps of
\G
. A fundamental property of these irreps is that any two non-equivalent irreps act on vector spaces that are mutually
orthogonal
. This implies that whenever we decompose symmetric vector spaces into their irreducible subspaces we are inherently decomposing the space into orthogonal
\G
-stable subspaces, which will greatly simplify numerical and theoretical analyses. Formally, these orthogonality relations are a consequence of Schur’s lemma, which we state below in its original form for the case of complex irreps and discuss its adaptation to real irreps.

Lemma H.10

(Schur’s Lemma for unitary (complex) representations
(
Knapp, 1986
, Prop 1.5)
)
.

Consider two
complex
Hilbert spaces,
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, endowed with the (
complex
) irreducible unitary representations
\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}:\G\mapsto\mathbb{U}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}:\G\mapsto\mathbb{U}({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
, respectively. Let
{\bm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
be a linear map commuting with the group actions, such that
{\bm{T}}\in\text{Homo}_{\G}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
. Then, if the irreducible representations are not equivalent, i.e.,
\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}\nsim\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}
, then
{\bm{T}}
is the trivial (or zero) map. Conversely, if
\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}\sim\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}
, then
{\bm{T}}
is a constant multiple of an isomorphism. Denoting
{\bm{I}}
as the identity operator, this can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}\nsim\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\bm{0}_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}={\bm{T}}{\bm{h}}\mid\forall\;{\bm{h}}\in{\mathcal{H}}

(39a)

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}\sim\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle{\bm{T}}=\alpha{\bm{U}},\alpha\in\mathbb{C},{\bm{U}}\cdot{\bm{U}}^{H}={\bm{I}}

(39b)

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}=\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle{\bm{T}}=\alpha{\bm{I}},\;\alpha\in\mathbb{C}

(39c)

The most common interpretation of Schur’s lemma is that whenever the irreps are equivalent,
\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}{}\sim\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}^{\prime}{}
, their associated spaces are isomorphic,
{\mathcal{H}}\sim{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
and
{\bm{T}}
is an element of the endomorphism space
\text{End}^{{\mathbb{C}}}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{H}}})
, with
\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}\sim{\mathcal{H}}\sim{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
(see
Definition

H.5
). Consequently, (
39b
) implies that the endomorphism space is one-dimensional, i.e.,
\dim(\text{End}^{{\mathbb{C}}}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}))=1
, with
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
denoting the only degree of freedom, and (
39c
) denotes the scenario in which the basis sets for the two spaces are identical.

However, this result holds only for
complex
irreducible representations and requires adaptation for the case of real irreducible representations. The main difference stems from the fact that if
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}:\G\to\mathbb{GL}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
is a real irrep, then the space of (real) endomorphisms,
\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
, is no longer one-dimensional, but rather it can be
1
,
2
, or
4
dimensional, depending on whether
\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{V}})
is isomorphic to the real algebra (
\R=\text{span}\{1\}
), complex algebra (
{\mathbb{C}}=\text{span}\{1,i\mid i^{2}=-1\}
), or quaternionic algebra (
{\mathbb{H}}=\text{span}\{1,i,j,k\mid i^{2}=j^{2}=k^{2}=-1\}
), respectively. Denoting by
\Psi:{\mathbb{K}}\to\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
the isomorphism of basis elements of
{\mathbb{K}}\in\{\R,{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{H}}\}
with the basis elements of
\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
, we can summarize the basis sets of the three cases as follows (see
Cesa et al. (2022, Appendix C)
for details):

\displaystyle\sim\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{V}})=\text{span}\{\Psi(1)={\bm{I}}_{\dim{\bar{\bm{\phi}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\}

(40)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\sim\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{V}})=\text{span}\{\Psi(1)=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}\end{bmatrix},\Psi(i)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}\\
{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}\}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\sim\text{End}_{\G}({\mathcal{V}})=\text{span}\left\{\begin{aligned} \Psi(1)&=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}\end{bmatrix},\Psi(i)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}\\
{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}\\
\Psi(j)&=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix},\Psi(k)=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}&{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&-{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\\
{\bm{I}}_{n}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}&\bm{0}\end{bmatrix}\end{aligned}\right\}

While this result might appear complex, its interpretation is straightforward: given a
\G
-stable irreducible space
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}
, the space of linear maps from the space to itself that preserve the symmetry structure consists of
linear transformations that scale all dimensions of
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}
uniformly, and possibly rotate or reflect the space
. Algebraically, this implies that any element of the algebra has a unique singular space, with a single singular value determined by the element’s coefficients in the basis of (
40
). We summarize this result in the following proposition.

Figure 20
:

Example of an endomorphism acting on a
\mathbb{C}_{3}
-stable irreducible
2
D space. The irreducible representation is of complex type, with endomorphism space
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}_{3}}(\R^{2})\sim\mathbb{C}=\text{span}\{1,i\}
, comprising all transformations that uniformly scale and rotate/reflect the plane.

Proposition H.11

(A real endomorphism has a single singular space)
.

Let
\G
be a compact symmetry group,
(\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}},\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
be an irreducible representation and its associated
\G
-stable space, and let

\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
denote the space endomorphism algebra.
Then every
{\bm{A}}\in\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
admits an
SVD

{\bm{A}}={\bm{U}}\,\gamma\,{\bm{I}}_{d}{\bm{V}}^{\top},

where
\gamma\in\R_{\geq 0}
is the
single
singular value, repeated with multiplicity

d=|\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}|=|{\mathcal{V}}_{{k}}|
.
The right singular basis
{\bm{V}}
can, without loss of generality, be taken as the canonical
orthonormal basis of
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}
, while the left singular basis is then

{\bm{U}}=\gamma^{-1}{\bm{A}}{\bm{V}}
, which is an orthogonal rotation/reflection of
{\bm{V}}
.

(
\R
)

Real case.

Any
{\bm{A}}\in\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
is of the form

{\bm{A}}=a\,\Psi(1)=a\,{\bm{I}}_{d},\qquad a\in\R.

Hence

{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}=a^{2}{\bm{I}}_{d},\qquad\sigma({\bm{A}})=\bigl\{|a|\bigr\}^{\times d}.

(
\mathbb{C}
)

Complex case.

Every element can be written as

{\bm{A}}=a\,\Psi(1)+b\,\Psi(i)=\begin{bmatrix}a{\bm{I}}_{n}&-b{\bm{I}}_{n}\\
b{\bm{I}}_{n}&\;\,a{\bm{I}}_{n}\end{bmatrix},\qquad a,b\in\R.

Using
\Psi(i)^{\top}=-\Psi(i)
and
\Psi(i)^{\top}\Psi(i)={\bm{I}}
,

{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}=(a^{2}+b^{2})\,{\bm{I}}_{d},\qquad\sigma({\bm{A}})=\bigl\{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\bigr\}^{\times d}.

(
{\mathbb{H}}
)

Quaternionic case.

Each element admits the expansion

{\bm{A}}=a\,\Psi(1)+b\,\Psi(i)+c\,\Psi(j)+d\,\Psi(k),\qquad a,b,c,d\in\R,

where
\Psi(i),\Psi(j),\Psi(k)
are the quaternionic structure matrices from
(
40
),
satisfying
\Psi(\alpha)^{\top}=-\Psi(\alpha)
,

\Psi(\alpha)^{2}=-{\bm{I}}
, and
\Psi(\alpha)^{\top}\Psi(\alpha)={\bm{I}}
,
with the usual anti-commutation rules.
Consequently,

{\bm{A}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}=(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}+d^{2})\,{\bm{I}}_{d},\qquad\sigma({\bm{A}})=\bigl\{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}+d^{2}}\bigr\}^{\times d}.

As an intuitive low-dimensional example, we can consider the case of a 2D rotational irrep of the cyclic group
\mathbb{C}_{3}
. The dimension of the irreducible
\G
-stable subspace is
\|bar{vsV}|=2
, and the irreducible representation is of complex type,
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}:\mathbb{C}_{3}\to\mathbb{GL}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})
, with
\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}})\sim{\mathbb{C}}=\text{span}\{1,i\}
denoting the space of all rotations/reflections and uniform scaling of the plane, see
Figure

20
.

H.1
Maps between Symmetric Vector Spaces

We will frequently study and use linear and non-linear maps between symmetric vector spaces. Our focus is on maps that preserve entirely or partially the group structure of the vector spaces. These types of maps can be classified as
\G
-equivariant,
\G
-invariant maps:

Definition H.12

(
\G
-equivariant and
\G
-invariant maps)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
be two vector spaces endowed with the same symmetry group
\G
, with the respective group actions
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}
. A map
f:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto{\mathcal{Y}}
is said to be
\G
-equivariant if it commutes with the group action, such that:

\small\begin{split}\small g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}}=g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}f({\bm{x}})&=f(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}},g\in\G.\\
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}(g)f({\bm{x}})&=f(\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{x}})\end{split}\hskip 18.49988pt\vbox{\hbox{$\iff$}}\hskip 18.49988pt\vbox{\hbox{
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 7.518pt\raise 4.97115pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18365pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.39442pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.518pt\raise-25.8177pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18365pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.46802pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}

(41a)

A specific case of
\G
-equivariant maps are the
\G
-invariant ones, which are maps that commute with the group action and have trivial output group actions
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}
such that
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}(g)={\bm{I}}
for all
g\in\G
. That is:

\small\begin{split}\small{\bm{y}}=g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}f({\bm{x}})&=f(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}},g\in\G.\\
{\bm{y}}=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}(g)f({\bm{x}})&=f(\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{x}})\end{split}\hskip 18.49988pt\vbox{\hbox{$\iff$}}\hskip 18.49988pt\vbox{\hbox{
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.518pt\raise 4.97115pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.18365pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.83388pt\raise-21.19443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.46802pt\raise-25.40094pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.93604pt\raise-15.39442pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.93604pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 30.46802pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern (41b)

Structure of
\G
-Equivariant Linear Maps

Definition H.13

(Homomorphism and Isomorphism)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
be two vector spaces endowed with the same symmetry group
\G
, with the respective group actions
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}:\G\times{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}:\G\times{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto{\mathcal{Y}}
. The spaces are said to be
\G
-homomorphic if there exists a linear map
{\mathbb{A}}:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto{\mathcal{Y}}
that commutes with the group action, such that

g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}({\bm{A}}{\bm{x}})={\bm{A}}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}})

for all
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
. They are said to be
\G
-isomorphic if the linear map is invertible. Graphically,
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
are
\G
-homomorphic or
\G
-isomorphic if the following diagrams commute:

\underbrace{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.94444pt\raise 5.19017pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 18.35005pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 39.65277pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\bm{A}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.65277pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.12001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\bm{A}}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.94444pt\raise-26.0876pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.31517pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}_{\text{Isomorphism}}\quad{\bm{A}}\in\text{Iso}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}}).

(42)

Here,
\text{Homo}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
denotes the space of
\G
-equivariant linear maps between
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, and
\text{Iso}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
denotes the space of
\G
-equivariant invertible linear maps between
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Proposition H.14

(Structure of
\G
-homomorphisms / interwiners /
\G
-equivariant linear maps)
.

Let
\G
be a compact group and
{\bm{A}}\in\text{Homo}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}})
be a
\G
-equivariant linear map between two (real)
\G
-symmetric vector spaces
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, with isotypic decompostions:

{\mathcal{X}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}\bm{\oplus}_{i=1}^{m^{x}_{{k}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{Y}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}\bm{\oplus}_{j=1}^{m^{y}_{{k}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{j},

where
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
denotes the number of isotypic subspaces, and
m^{x}_{{k}}
and
m^{y}_{{k}}
denote the multiplicities of the irreducible representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}:\G\to\mathbb{GL}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{k}})
in
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
, respectively. Each
{\mathcal{X}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{j}
is isometrically isomorphic to
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{k}}
(see
Theorem

H.17
). Hence, in the isotypic bases, the map
{\bm{A}}
decomposes block-diagonally into
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
blocks corresponding to homomorphisms between isotypic subspaces of the same type, that is:

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\qquad\text{where}\quad{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\in\text{Homo}_{\G}({\mathcal{X}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}},{\mathcal{Y}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}).

Furthermore, the map
{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
decomposes into
m^{x}_{{k}}\times m^{y}_{{k}}
blocks of endomorphisms of the irreducible subspace
\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{k}}
. That is:

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{1,1}&\cdots&{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{1,m^{x}_{{k}}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{m^{y}_{{k}},1}&\cdots&{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{m^{y}_{{k}},m^{x}_{{k}}}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{where}\quad{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{i,j}\in\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{k}}),\forall\;i\in[1,m^{y}_{{k}}],j\in[1,m^{x}_{{k}}].

Consequently, depending of the type of irreducible representation
{\mathbb{K}}\in\{{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{H}}\}
, each sub-block is constrained to be in the span of the corresponding basis elements in
Equation

40
. Consequenly, if we denote by
{\mathbb{B}}
the basis set of
{\mathbb{K}}
, we have that the map
{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
can be expressed in tensor product form as:

\displaystyle{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}=\sum_{b\in{\mathbb{B}}}\bm{\Theta}_{b}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\otimes\Psi_{k}(b),\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{\Theta}_{b}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\in\R^{m^{y}_{{k}}\times m^{x}_{{k}}},\Psi_{k}:{\mathbb{B}}\to\text{End}_{\G}(\bar{{\mathcal{V}}}_{{k}})

(43)

With
[\bm{\Theta}_{b}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}]_{i,j}={\langle{\bm{A}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{i,j},\Psi_{k}(b)\rangle}
denoting the basis expansion coefficient of the
i
-th,
j
-th endomorphism sub-block with the basis element
\Psi_{k}(b)
.

H.2
Isotypic Decomposition and Disentangled Representations

We now have all the necessary tools to decompose symmetric vector spaces into their fundamental building blocks: irreducible
\G
-stable subspaces (
Definition

H.7
). This decomposition forms the theoretical foundation for disentangled representations, a concept introduced by
Higgins et al. (2018)
in the context of group theory and generalized here within the framework of representation theory.

By Maschke’s theorem
(
Knapp, 1986
)
, we have that irreducible representations are the fundamental building blocks of the representations of a compact symmetry group
\G
, given that any group representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}:\G\to\mathbb{GL}({\mathcal{X}})
can be decomposed into a direct sum of irreducible representations,
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}\sim\bm{\oplus}_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{i}$}}
, where each
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}_{i}$}}
is isomorphic to one of the group’s
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}\leq|\G|
irreducible representations (
Definitions

H.9
and
H.8
).

This decomposition will play a crucial role in facilitating numerical and theoretical analysis of operations on symmetric vector spaces. Therefore, we will frequenlt choose a convenient basis of the symmetric vector space which readily exposes this decomposition, termed isotypic basis. For the sake of generality we consider below the more general case of (finite and infinite dimensional) separable Hilbert spaces, which will enable us to extend this results to function spaces.

Definition H.15

(Isotypic Basis)
.

Let
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{H}}$}}:\G\to\mathbb{U}({\mathcal{H}})
be a unitary group representation of a compact group
\G
on a separable Hilbert Space
{\mathcal{H}}
. The representation is said to be defined in an
isotypic basis
if it is defined by a direct sum of irreducible representations grouped by their type, that is, if:

\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{H}}$}}=\bm{\oplus}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}\bm{\oplus}_{{p}=1}^{m_{{k}}}\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}},

(44)

where
\{\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}:\G\to\mathbb{U}(\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}_{k})\}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}
are the
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}\leq|\G|
irreducible representations of
\G
, and
m_{{k}}\leq\infty
is the multiplicity (i.e., number of copies) of the irrep type
{k}
in the representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{H}}$}}
.

Remark H.16
.

Note that multiple isotypic bases exist for a given representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{H}}$}}
, as both the irrep ordering and each irrep’s multiplicity ordering can be arbitrarily permuted.

The utility of an isotypic basis stems from Schur’s orthogonality relations (
Lemma

H.10
), which ensure that any symmetric vector space decomposes into at most
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}
orthogonal subspaces.

Theorem H.17

(Isotypic decomposition of symmetric Hilbert spaces
(
Knapp, 1986
)
)
.

Let
\G
be a compact group and
{\mathcal{H}}
a separable Hilbert space with a unitary group representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{H}}$}}:\G\rightarrow\mathbb{U}({\mathcal{H}})
. Then we can identify
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}\leq|\G|
irreducible representations
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}:\G\rightarrow\mathbb{U}(\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}_{k})
that allow us to decompose
{\mathcal{H}}
into a sum of orthogonal subspaces, denoted
isotypic subspaces
:
{\mathcal{H}}=\bigoplus_{1\leq{k}\leq{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}^{\perp}{\mathcal{H}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}
where each
{\mathcal{H}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}=\bigoplus_{{p}=1}^{m_{k}}{\mathcal{H}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}_{p}
is the sum of at most
m_{{k}}\leq\infty
countably many subspaces isometrically isomorphic to
\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}_{k}
.

Remark H.18
.

In practice, the isotypic decomposition of any finite-dimensional symmetric Hilbert space is obtained through a unitary or orthogonal change of basis. This change of basis can be computed numerically using Dixon’s reduction method for finite-dimensional unitary (complex) representations
(
Dixon, 1970
)
, with minor additional logic for the orthogonal (real) case. This method is implemented in
symm_learning

(
Ordonez Apraez, 2026
)
through the
cplx_isotypic_decomposition
function.

For
\G
-equivariant
NN
backbone architecture, the torch module
symm_learning.nn.Change2DisentangledBasis
computes the required change of basis to an isotypic basis and applies it to the backbone output representations, thereby producing the disentangled representations used in this work and explain in detail next.

Disentangled Representations

The concept of isotypic decomposition is intricately linked to the idea of disentangled representations, introduced by
Higgins et al. (2018)
in the representation learning literature, restated below for completeness.

Definition H.19

(Disentangled representation (
Higgins et al. (2018)
))
.

A vector representation is called a disentangled representation with respect to a particular decomposition of a symmetry group into subgroups, if it decomposes into independent subspaces, where each subspace is affected by the action of a single subgroup, and the actions of all other subgroups leave the subspace unaffected.

Note that the independent subspaces of
Definition

H.19
refer to the orthogonal isotypic subspaces
\{{\mathcal{H}}^{{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}}\}_{{k}=1}^{{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}}
, each of which is acted upon by a unique
quotient group
5
5

5

The original definition of disentangled representations refers to subgroups, but in general the quotient grup
\G^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}
need not be a
subgroup
of
\G
.
defined by the kernel of the irrep acting on that isotypic subspace:

\G^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}=\G/{\mathbb{N}}_{{k}},\quad\text{where}\quad{\mathbb{N}}_{{k}}:=\text{ker}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}})=\{g\in\G\mid\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}(g)={\bm{I}}_{d_{{k}}}\}.

(45)

Where each
\G^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}
is a well defined group of cosets generated by the normal subgroup
{\mathbb{N}}_{{k}}
. In practice, each
\G^{{\scalebox{0.6}{$({k})$}}}
is isomorphic to the effective (matrix) group encoded by each irreducible representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${k}$}}:\G\mapsto\mathbb{U}(\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}_{k})
.

Appendix I
Representation Theory of Symmetric Function Spaces

Figure 21
:

Left:
Diagram of the group action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}
on functions
f_{1}(x)=x^{2}+c
and
f_{2}(x)=x^{3}
defined on the domain
{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathbb{R}
endowed with the reflectional symmetry group
\G:=\mathbb{C}_{2}=\{e,g_{s}\}
, with the reflection action acting on the domain by
g_{s}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}x=-x
and on the function space
{\mathcal{F}}:=\{f\mid f:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\R\}
by
[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f]({\bm{x}})=f(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}})=f(-{\bm{x}})
. Hence we have that
f_{1}
is a
\G
-invariant function,
g_{s}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{1}(x)=f_{1}(x)
and
f_{2}
a
\G
-equivariant function
g_{s}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{2}(x)=-x^{3}
.

Center:
Diagram representing the action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}
on the (arbitrarily chosen) function
f({\bm{x}})=\mathcal{N}({\bm{x}};{\bm{c}}_{1},2)+\mathcal{N}({\bm{x}};{\bm{c}}_{2},1)
defined over the symmetric domain
{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the cyclic symmetry group
\G=\mathbb{C}_{3}=\{e,g_{120},g_{240}\}
and group action
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}(g){\bm{x}}={\bm{R}}_{g}{\bm{x}}
, where
{\bm{R}}_{g}
is a rotation matrix in 2D. Here,
g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f
is equivalent to evaluating
f
on a domain rotated by
-120^{\circ}
. The same holds for
g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f
. Note that the
z
-offsets are added for visualization purposes.

Right:
Diagram representing the action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}
on the function
z\in\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
, defined to be a member of the finite-dimensional symmetric function space
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}:=\text{span}({\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}$}})
, constructed from a basis set composed of the group orbit of the (arbitrarily chosen) function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, that is
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}$}}:=\G f=\{f,g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f,g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f\}
. This function space is
\G
-stable by construction, since
\G{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}$}}={\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}$}}
. Note that the
z
-offsets are added for visualization purposes.

In this section, we study symmetry group actions on infinite-dimensional function spaces and specify the conditions needed to approximate these spaces in finite dimensions. Specifically, given a set
{\mathcal{X}}
with a compact symmetry group
\G
acting via
(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}})
(
Definition

H.2
), the space of scalar-valued functions on
{\mathcal{X}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}=\{f\mid f:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\}
, becomes a symmetric function space. The action of a symmetry transformation on a function is defined as:

Definition I.1

(Group action on a function space)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a set endowed with the symmetry group
\G
, and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the space of scalar-valued functions on
{\mathcal{X}}
. The (left) action of
\G
on a function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
is defined as the composition of
f
with the inverse of the group element
g^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}(\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}):&\G\times{\mathcal{F}}&\longrightarrow&{\mathcal{F}}\\
&(g,f)&\longrightarrow&[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f]({\bm{x}}):=[f\mathrel{{\circ}}g^{-1}]({\bm{x}})=f(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}),\quad\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}.\end{matrix}

(46a)

In other words, the point-wise evaluation of
f
on a
g^{-1}
-transformed set
{\mathcal{X}}
is equivalent to the evaluation of the transformed function
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f\in{\mathcal{F}}
on the original set
{\mathcal{X}}
(see simple examples in
Figure

21
). Any function space that is stable under the group action
Equation

46a
is refereed to as a symmetric function space. Note that this action is compatible with the group composition and identity element
e\in\G
, such that the following properties hold:

Identity:

\displaystyle\quad e\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f(\cdot)=f(\cdot),

(46b)

Associativity:

\displaystyle\quad[(g_{2}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{1})\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f](\cdot)=[g_{2}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}[g_{1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f]](\cdot),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;g_{1},g_{2}\in\G.

(46c)

Remark I.2
.

From an algebraic perspective, the inversion
g^{-1}
(
contragredient representation
) emerges to ensure that the associativity property of the group action (
Equation

46c
) holds:

\displaystyle\small[(g_{2}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{1})\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f]({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=[g_{2}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}[g_{1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f]]({\bm{x}}),\qquad\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle f((g_{2}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{1})^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=[g_{1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f](g_{2}^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}})=f(g_{1}^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}(g_{2}^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle f((g_{2}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{1})^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=f((g_{1}\mathrel{{\circ}}g_{2})^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}}).

In the context of this work, we will study the scenario where the function space
{\mathcal{F}}
is a separable Hilbert space and the group action of
\G
on
{\mathcal{F}}
is unitary, i.e., it preserves the inner product of the function space. This setup is crucial to enable us to approximate
{\mathcal{F}}
and the group action on
{\mathcal{F}}
in finite dimensions.

I.1
Unitary Group Representation on Function Spaces

Assume our symmetric set
{\mathcal{X}}
is endowed with a measure space structure
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}},P_{{\mathbf{x}}})
, where
P_{{\mathbf{x}}}:\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}\mapsto\R
is the space measure. Then, consider a function space with a separable Hilbert space structure
{\mathcal{F}}:=\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}$}{{\mathcal{X}},\R}
, and inner product
{\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f_{1}({\bm{x}})f_{2}({\bm{x}})P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}})
for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Then, the action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}
of the group
\G
on the function space
{\mathcal{F}}
is termed unitary if it preserves the inner product of the function space:

\displaystyle{\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}

\displaystyle={\langle g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{1},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{2}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\forall\;f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathcal{F}},g\in\G

(47)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{1}({\bm{x}})f_{2}({\bm{x}})P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{1})({\bm{x}})(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{2})({\bm{x}})P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{1}(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}})f_{2}(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\bm{x}})P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}}f_{1}({\bm{x}})f_{2}({\bm{x}})P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}d{\bm{x}}).

That is, the group action is unitary if
P_{{\mathbf{x}}}
is a
\G
-invariant measure
P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}d{\bm{x}})=P_{{\mathbf{x}}}(d{\bm{x}}),\;\forall\;g\in\G,d{\bm{x}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
. Note that an
\G
-invariant measure (and inner product) exists whenever
\G
is finite, because for any measure
\eta:\Sigma_{\Omega}\mapsto\R
, we can use the group-average trick to obtain one, given by
P_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbb{X}})=\Sigma_{g\in\G}\eta(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}{\mathbb{X}})
.
6
6

6

Such a
\G
-invariant measure exists for any (finite or continuous) compact group. See
discussion
.

The importance of the Hilbert space structure is that it enables the definition of a unitary group representation. Unitary representations have a well-studied modular structure that allows their decomposition (
Theorem

H.17
) into
\G
-stable subspaces (
Definition

H.7
), which is crucial for approximating symmetric function spaces using a finite set of basis elements. Let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}=\{\phi_{i}\mid\phi_{i}\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an orthogonal basis for the function space
{\mathcal{F}}=\text{span}({\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{${\mathcal{F}}$}})
, so that any function
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
can be represented by its basis expansion coefficients
{\bm{\alpha}}=[{\langle\phi_{i}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}{f}]_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, since
f_{{\bm{\alpha}}}({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\langle\phi_{i},f\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\phi_{i}({\bm{x}})
. In this basis, the group action of
\G
on
{\mathcal{F}}
defines a unitary group representation mapping group elements to unitary linear integral operators on
{\mathcal{F}}
, which can be expressed in matrix form.

Definition I.3

(Unitary group representation on a function space)
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}=\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}$}{{\mathcal{X}},\R}
be a separable Hilbert space of scalar-valued functions on a set
{\mathcal{X}}
endowed with the symmetry group
\G
. Let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}
be an orthogonal basis set spanning
{\mathcal{F}}
. Then, the group action of
\G
on
{\mathcal{F}}
(
Definition

I.1
) defines a unitary group representation mapping group elements to unitary linear integral operators on
{\mathcal{F}}
:

\small\begin{matrix}\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}:&\G&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{U}({\mathcal{F}})\\
&g&\longrightarrow&\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}(g)\end{matrix},\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\text{s.t. }\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}(g)^{*}=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}(g^{-1}).

(48)

Each unitary operator
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}(g):{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}
admits an infinite-dimensional matrix representation with entries
[\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}(g)]_{i,j}:={\langle\hat{f}_{i},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}\hat{f}_{j}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
, which characterize how the group action transforms the chosen basis functions. Consequently, once the group representation for a chosen basis set is defined, the group action on a function
f_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
can be expressed as an (infinite-dimensional) matrix transformation of its basis expansion coefficients
{\bm{\alpha}}
, given by:

\displaystyle f_{{\bm{\alpha}}}](\cdot)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\langle\hat{f}_{i},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}f_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\hat{f}_{i}(\cdot)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}{\langle\hat{f}_{i},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{F}}$}}}}\hat{f}_{j}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\underbrace{{\langle\hat{f}_{j},f\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}}_{\alpha_{j}}\bigg)\hat{f}_{i}(\cdot).

(49)

Example I.4

(Isotypic decomposition of symmetric function space)
.

Let
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},P_{{\mathbf{x}}})
be a symmetric 2D measure space with domain
{\mathcal{X}}\sim\R^{2}
and cyclic symmetry group
\G:=\mathbb{C}_{3}=\{e,g_{120},g_{240}\}
, acting on the 2D plane by
120^{\circ}
rotations (
Figure

22
). Define the finite-dimensional function space
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}\subset\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
with basis
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}=\{\phi,g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi,g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi\}
, where
\phi\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
is an arbitrary measurable function (
Figure

22
-left). In this basis, the group action
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}}}
for any function
z_{\bm{\alpha}}\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
is given by the regular representation
\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}=\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\text{reg}$}}
acting on the coefficient vector
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\R^{3}
(
Figure

7
-right).

\small[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}}}z_{\bm{\alpha}}](\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}{\langle\phi_{i},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}}}z_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle}_{P_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\phi_{i}(\cdot)\equiv(\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\text{reg}$}}(g){\bm{\alpha}})^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\phi(\cdot)\\
g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi(\cdot)\\
g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi(\cdot)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\text{reg}$}}(g)=\begin{cases}{\bm{I}}_{3},&\text{if }g=e\\
\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{bmatrix},&\text{if }g=g_{120}\\
\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{bmatrix},&\text{if }g=g_{240}\end{cases}

(50)

The group
\mathbb{C}_{3}
possesses two types of (real-valued) irreducible representations,
{n_{{\text{\rm iso}}}}=2
: the trivial irreducible representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\text{inv}}$}}
and a 2D rotation representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nicefrac{{2\pi}}{{3}}$}}
, defined by:

\small\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\text{inv}}$}}(g)={\bm{I}}_{1},\forall\;g\in\mathbb{C}_{3},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nicefrac{{2\pi}}{{3}}$}}(g)=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\theta)&-\sin(\theta)\\
\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t. }\theta=\begin{cases}0^{\circ},&\text{if }g=e\\
120^{\circ}&\text{if }g=g_{120}\\
240^{\circ}&\text{if }g=g_{240}\end{cases}

(51)

Applying the appropriate change of basis, we decompose the regular representation into a direct sum of the group’s irreducible representations:

\bm{\rho}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\text{reg}$}}={\bm{Q}}(\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\text{inv}}$}}\oplus\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nicefrac{{2\pi}}{{3}}$}}){\bm{Q}}^{-1},

where
{\bm{Q}}
transitions from the regular basis to the isotypic basis of
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
. Since
\mathbb{C}_{3}
is abelian,
{\bm{Q}}
corresponds to the linear map defining the Fourier transform.

By
Theorem

H.17
, this results in the orthogonal decomposition of the finite-dimensional function space into two orthogonal subspaces;
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}=\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\text{inv}}\oplus^{\perp}\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{(2)}
, where
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\text{inv}}
denotes the 1-dimensional subspace of
\G
-invariant functions (
\G^{(0)}=\{e\}
), and
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{(2)}
is the 2-dimensional subspace with group actions defined by the 2D irreducible representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nicefrac{{2\pi}}{{3}}$}}
(
\G^{(1)}=\mathbb{C}_{3}
). We can construct the basis set in the isotypic basis given:

\small{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}^{\text{\rm iso}}={\bm{Q}}\begin{bmatrix}\phi(\cdot)\\
g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi(\cdot)\\
g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi(\cdot)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}u^{\text{inv}}(\cdot)\\
u^{(2)}_{1}(\cdot)\\
u^{(2)}_{2}(\cdot)\end{bmatrix}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{s.t. }{\bm{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{3}}}&\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{3}}}&\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{3}}}\\
\nicefrac{{2}}{{\sqrt{6}}}&-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{6}}}&-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{6}}}\\
0&\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}}&-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}}\end{bmatrix}

(52)

The new basis functions in the isotypic basis are depicted in
Figure

22
-right, and elucidate that the symmetry constraints on this
3
-dimensional function space, result in
m=2
unique functions, each associated with a unique irreducible representation.

Assuming
P_{{\mathbf{x}}}
is a
\G
-invariant probability measure, we compute the expected value of each basis function. In the regular basis, functions related by a symmetry transformation share the same expected value, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\phi=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi
for all
g\in\mathbb{C}_{3}
. In the isotypic basis, functions lacking a
\G
-invariant component (i.e.,
u^{(2)}_{1},u^{(2)}_{2}
) are centered:
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}u^{(2)}_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{x}}}u^{(2)}_{2}=0
. In our example this constraint becomes clear from the nature of the change of basis
{\bm{Q}}
.

Equation

52
.

Figure 22
:

Visualization of the basis functions in the finite-dimensional symmetric function space
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
from
Example

I.4
.

Left
: Depiction of the basis functions in the regular basis
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}=\{\phi,g_{120}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi,g_{240}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$$}}}}\phi\}
, generated by the action of the cyclic group
\mathbb{C}_{3}
on an arbitrary function
\phi\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}
.

Right
: Depiction of the basis functions in the isotypic basis
{\mathbb{I}}_{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}^{\text{\rm iso}}=\{u^{\text{inv}},u^{(2)}_{1},u^{(2)}_{2}\}
, obtained via the change of basis matrix
{\bm{Q}}
.
The first basis function
u^{\text{inv}}
corresponds to the
\G
-invariant subspace
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{\text{inv}}
and is visually invariant under the action of
\mathbb{C}_{3}
on
{\mathcal{X}}
.
The other two basis functions
u^{(2)}_{1}
,
u^{(2)}_{2}
are constrained to span a
\G
-stable subspace of

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

, denoted by
\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{(2)}
that transform according to the irreducible representation
\bar{\bm{\rho}}_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\nicefrac{{2\pi}}{{3}}$}}
. Meaning for any function
f\in\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}^{(2)}
, the group action
g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{${\mathcal{F}}$}_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathbf{x}}$}}$}}}}f
can be computed by a linear transformation of its basis expansion coefficients.

Appendix J
\G
-Equivariant Linear Integral Operators

This section gives an overview of
\G
-equivariant linear integral operators between symmetric function spaces. We define these operators, discuss their properties, and specify conditions under which they commute with group actions. In
Section

J.1
we examine their infinite-dimensional matrix form and the resulting algebraic constraints from
\G
-equivariance. In
Section

J.2
we then show how to exploit these constraints in a finite-rank approximation.

Let
\G
be a compact group acting on two measure spaces
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}},\mux)
and
({\mathcal{Y}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}},\muy)
via the group actions
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}
and
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}
(see
Definition

H.2
). Assume that the measures
\mux
and
\muy
are
\G
-invariant, i.e.,
\mux(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathbb{B}})=\mux({\mathbb{B}})
and
\muy(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\mathbb{A}})=\muy({\mathbb{A}})
for all
g\in\G
,
{\mathbb{B}}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
, and
{\mathbb{A}}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}}
(see
Definition

H.12
).

Let

\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}=\{f:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\R\mid\left\|f\right\|_{\mux}<+\infty\}\quad\text{and}\quad\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}=\{h:{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\R\mid\left\|h\right\|_{\muy}<+\infty\}

be the Hilbert spaces of square-integrable functions with respect to
\mux
and
\muy
, respectively. Since
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
have a
\G
-action, the spaces

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and

\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}

inherit group actions defined by

[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}$}}}}f]({\bm{x}})=f(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}}),\quad[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}$}}}}h]({\bm{y}})=h(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}}),

for all
f\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}
and
h\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
(see
Definition

I.1
).

We consider linear integral operators
{\mathsf{T}}:\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}\mapsto\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
defined by

\small h({\bm{y}})=[{\mathsf{T}}f]({\bm{y}})=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}}),

(53)

where
k:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\R
is the kernel function of
{\mathsf{T}}
. In this work we focus on those operators whose kernels are
\G
-invariant such operators are called
\G
-equivariant.

Definition J.1

(
\G
-equivariant linear intergral operators)
.

Let
({\mathcal{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}},\mux)
and
({\mathcal{Y}},\Sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}},\muy)
be two measure spaces endowed with group actions
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}
and
\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}
and
\G
-invariant measures
\mux
and
\muy
for a given compact symmetry group
\G
. Let
{\mathsf{T}}:\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}\mapsto\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}
be a linear integral operator between the spaces of square-integrable functions defined on the two measure spaces. The operator
{\mathsf{T}}
is said to be
\G
-equivariant if it commutes with the group action, that is
\forall\;f\in\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{},g\in\G
and
{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{Y}}
:

\displaystyle[{\mathsf{T}}[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{x}}}$}{}$}}}}f]]({\bm{y}})

\displaystyle=[g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}$}}}}[{\mathsf{T}}f]]({\bm{y}})

(54a)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})f(g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{$\scalebox{0.85}{$\mathcal{L}^{2}_{{\mathbf{y}}}$}{}$}}}}\left(\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}})\right)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}})\hskip 18.49988pt\text{s.t. }g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\mathcal{X}}:={\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}\pmd({\bm{x}},g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})f({\bm{x}})\,\mux(d{\bm{x}})\hskip 18.49988pt\text{s.t. }\mux(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}d{\bm{x}})=\mux(d{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle k(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})

\displaystyle=\pmd({\bm{x}},g^{-1}\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})\iff k(g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{X}}$}}}}{\bm{x}},g\mathrel{{\triangleright_{\scalebox{0.65}{${\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}{\bm{y}})=\pmd({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}).

(54b)

Notice that the
\G
-equivariance of the operator
{\mathsf{T}}
is linked to the
\G
-invariance of its kernel function, which is required to satisfy
Equation

54b
.

Multiple approaches exist to parameterize and approximate linear integral operators with finite
resources
(
Kovachki et al., 2023
, sec. 4)
. Here, we assume that both the input and output function
spaces are separable Hilbert spaces, so that the operator can be represented as an infinite-dimensional
